| 1144 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Millions of people don't have clean water nearby. Today, it's Priya's turn. Priya is from Asia, and her town is surrounded by green hills and rice fields. In the villages around her town, there aren't many schools or libraries. So every Saturday, a big, bright library bus drives from village to village. It is painted with elephants, stars and flowers, and it is full of books. Children run to meet it when they hear its happy horn. Orbit says that around the world, millions of children can't go to school. Some live too far away, and some have to help at home. Riko thinks for a moment and says, Every child should be able to go to school. Say it with me. Every child should be able to go to school. Wonderful! Priya shows us her blog post on a big screen. Let's read it. "Hi! This is the story of our library bus. Every Saturday morning, the bus leaves my town at eight o'clock. Our teachers and volunteers drive it along the hill roads to five villages. At each stop, they open the side doors, and shelves full of picture books, comics and science books appear. Children can borrow two books for one week. Some children are learning to read for the first time, so volunteers read stories aloud under a big tree. Last month, a girl told me she wants to be a doctor. Then she borrowed a book about the human body. Books open doors. I hope every child can have a chance to learn." How many books can children borrow? Two books for one week. What does the girl want to be? A doctor. Birimo jumps up and says, I'm playing the library bus! Beep beep! Books for everyone! Now, phonics. Endangered is en plus danger plus ed, which means in danger. Solar comes from sol, which means sun, plus ar. For the moon, we say lunar. Reusable is re plus use plus able, but drop the e in use. Next time, we'll make a world map of challenges together. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Millions of people don't have clean water nearby. Today, it's Priya's turn. Priya is from Asia, and her town is surrounded by green hills and rice fields. In the villages around her town, there aren't many schools or libraries. So every Saturday, a big, bright library bus drives from village to village. It is painted with elephants, stars and flowers, and it is full of books. Children run to meet it when they hear its happy horn. Orbit says that around the world, millions of children can't go to school. Some live too far away, and some have to help at home. Riko thinks for a moment and says, Every child should be able to go to school. Say it with me. Every child should be able to go to school. Wonderful! Priya shows us her blog post on a big screen. Let's read it. "Hi! This is the story of our library bus. Every Saturday morning, the bus leaves my town at eight o'clock. Our teachers and volunteers drive it along the hill roads to five villages. At each stop, they open the side doors, and shelves full of picture books, comics and science books appear. Children can borrow two books for one week. Some children are learning to read for the first time, so volunteers read stories aloud under a big tree. Last month, a girl told me she wants to be a doctor. Then she borrowed a book about the human body. Books open doors. I hope every child can have a chance to learn." How many books can children borrow? Two books for one week. What does the girl want to be? A doctor. Birimo jumps up and says, I'm playing the library bus! Beep beep! Books for everyone! Now, phonics. Endangered is en plus danger plus e, d, which means in danger. Solar comes from sol, which means sun, plus a, r. For the moon, we say lunar. Reusable is re plus use plus able, but drop the e in use. Next time, we'll make a world map of challenges together. | B+C | bare 'ed' is read as the name Ed, but in endangered it is only a d sound; spell the letters; bare 'ar' is read as 'are' or the letter R; spell the letters (W31 precedent) |

| 1145 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Every child should be able to go to school. Today, we're making a world map of challenges. Orbit rolls out a giant paper map across the round table. We have colorful pins and cards. Blue pins are for clean water, green pins are for food, and yellow pins are for learning. Kiko writes the cards in her neatest handwriting, Haru and Riko push in the pins, and Birimo draws tiny pictures of buckets, books and bread. Ms. Emma helps us check every card, so each fact is true. Soon, the map is covered with pins, from the hills of Asia to the beaches of Oceania. Haru steps back and points at the green pins. Look! They're everywhere. This challenge affects many countries. Say it with me. This challenge affects many countries. Fantastic! Then we present our map to the six children. Let's read our presentation. "Hello, everyone. This is our world map of challenges. The blue pins show places where people are working to bring clean water closer to homes, like Amina's new well. The green pins show food waste. About one third of food is wasted, and this challenge affects many countries, including ours. The yellow pins show places where children are getting new chances to learn, like Priya's library bus. Each challenge is big, but each pin also has a card with good news. People are building wells, sharing food and driving books to villages. Thank you for listening. We hope you can help, too. Do you have any questions?" What do the yellow pins show? Places where children are getting new chances to learn. What is on each card? Good news. Everyone claps, and Orbit spins so fast that he almost wobbles. Every voice counts! Suddenly, the first lantern begins to glow, softly at first, then bright and golden. The Lantern of Knowing lit up! We know so much more about our world now. Now, phonics. Let's review. Environment has an n before the m. Recycle is re plus cycle, which means circle again. Solar comes from sol, which means sun. Next time, we'll visit Mateo's uncle on his farm. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Every child should be able to go to school. Today, we're making a world map of challenges. | 1146 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, This challenge affects many countries. Today, Mateo from South America brings us a surprise. Orbit opens a round window in the hall, and through it we can see his uncle's farm, high in the green mountains. Mateo's uncle waves at us from a field of carrots. He pulls up a carrot with two legs, like a little dancer. Then he pulls up another one shaped like a heart, and a tiny one that looks like a smiling face. Birimo laughs. Can carrots dance? Mateo's uncle laughs, too, but then he explains that funny-shaped carrots are hard to sell. Many shops only want straight ones. Ms. Emma says, Let's think from a different point of view（見かた・立場）. A point of view is the way someone sees a problem from their own place. Kiko thinks about all that hard work and says, From the farmer's point of view, every carrot is precious. Say it with me. From the farmer's point of view, every carrot is precious. Super! Mateo shares an interview with his uncle. Let's read it. "Mateo: Uncle, how long does it take to grow a carrot? Uncle: About three months. I plant the seeds, water them, pull the weeds and protect them from hungry rabbits. Mateo: What happens to carrots with funny shapes? Uncle: Some shops don't want them, because they think customers won't buy them. But they taste just as sweet as straight ones. Mateo: So what do you do with them? Uncle: I sell some at the village market, and some become carrot soup and juice. Mateo: What do you wish people knew? Uncle: That a carrot is a carrot, whatever its shape. From my point of view, every carrot is precious." How long does it take to grow a carrot? About three months. Do funny-shaped carrots taste different? No, they taste just as sweet. Now, phonics. Let's say big numbers. One million has six zeros. Ten million has seven zeros. One hundred million has eight zeros. Remember, we say two million, not two millions. Next time, Sam will tell us about his aunt's grocery shop. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, This challenge affects many countries. Today, Mateo from South America brings us a surprise. | 1147 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, From the farmer's point of view, every carrot is precious. Today, we look at food from a shop's point of view. Sam from North America tells us about his aunt. She runs a small grocery shop on a busy street, with baskets of apples, bananas and tomatoes out front. Every morning, she opens the doors at seven, and her neighbors stop by for fresh bread and fruit. Birimo asks, Why would a shop throw away good food? Sam explains that many customers pick only the shiniest, roundest apples. The ones with little spots stay on the shelf. Also, every package has a date label, and some people won't buy anything when the date is close. Riko nods and says, Shops throw away food because customers want perfect fruit. Say it with me. Shops throw away food because customers want perfect fruit. Nice job! But Sam's aunt had an idea. Let's read the notice in her shop window. "Hello, shoppers! Meet our new Wonky Veggie Boxes! Inside each box, you'll find carrots with two legs, bumpy potatoes, tiny apples and tomatoes in funny shapes. They look a little different, but they taste just as delicious. Each box costs less than regular vegetables, so you save money, too. And please remember, a best-before date tells you when food is at its best. Food is often still good after that date, so look, smell and ask a grown-up. Every box you buy helps farmers and keeps good food from being thrown away. Ask us about this week's box. Thank you for shopping with us!" What's inside a Wonky Veggie Box? Carrots with two legs, bumpy potatoes, tiny apples and funny-shaped tomatoes. What does a best-before date tell you? When food is at its best. Sam says the boxes sell out every Friday, and his aunt throws away much less food now! Now, phonics. Here are even bigger numbers. One billion has nine zeros. About eight billion people live on Earth, so eight billion is a very big number. Two point five million means two and a half million. Next time, Liam will tell us about plastic and the sea. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, From the farmer's point of view, every carrot is precious. | 1147 | `teacher.segments[9].subtitleJa` | かずの じかん！ もっと 大きいよ！ one billion は 1 の あとに 0 が 9 こ。地球には やく eight billion、80 おくにんの 人が すんで いるよ。わあ！ | かずの じかん！ もっと 大きいよ！ one billion は 1 の あとに 0 が 9 こ。地球には やく eight billion、80 おく人が すんで いるよ。わあ！ | objective (JA) | '80 おくにんの 人' doubles 人 (eight billion people people); 80 おく人 is natural |
| 1148 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Shops throw away food because customers want perfect fruit. Today, Liam from Oceania holds up a plastic water bottle. He says, Plastic is amazing in many ways. It's light, it's cheap, and it keeps food fresh. Then he shows us a photo of a beach near his home, with bottle caps and plastic bags in the sand. Ms. Emma teaches us a new linking word. When you connect a good point and a bad point, you can use however. Haru tries it. Plastic is useful. However, it can harm the sea. Say it with me. Plastic is useful. However, it can harm the sea. Perfect! Birimo gasps. Oh no! The sea is not a trash can! Liam nods. That's right, and the good news is that many people are helping. Liam's class decided to do something about it. Let's read his beach clean-up report. "Our Beach Clean-Up Report. Last Saturday, our class and our teachers held a beach clean-up. Everyone wore gloves, and the grown-ups picked up anything sharp. In two hours, we filled twelve big bags. We found bottle caps, straws, food wrappers and one very old flip-flop. Plastic is useful, because it keeps our sandwiches fresh. However, when plastic ends up in the sea, turtles and seabirds can mistake it for food. After the clean-up, we made a promise. We will bring reusable bottles to school and use lunch boxes instead of plastic bags. The beach looked beautiful, and a little crab came out to say thank you!" How many bags did the class fill? Twelve big bags. What did the class promise to bring to school? Reusable bottles. Birimo walks sideways and says, I'm playing the crab! Thank you, friends! Now, phonics. Let's say parts of a whole. One of two equal parts is half. One of three equal parts is one third. Two of three equal parts is two thirds. Thirds has an s, because there are two of them. Remember, about one third of food is wasted. Next time, we'll ask everyone at the hall some questions. | Hello, everyone! | 1149 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Plastic is useful. However, it can harm the sea. Today, Orbit asks us to do a survey. We ask the children and teachers at the hall the same three questions about recycling, water and food. Riko and Kiko hold clipboards, Haru counts the answers, and Birimo makes a very tall tally chart. In the end, we ask forty people. Nadia and Mateo even help us count the last answers, and everyone is very kind and patient. Haru looks at the results and shouts, Everyone recycles! But Ms. Emma smiles. Careful! Not everyone answered yes. Haru laughs. Oops! You're right. When we aren't completely sure, we can say it seems that. Riko tries it. It seems that more people are recycling. Say it with me. It seems that more people are recycling. Great! We can also say, This might mean that something is true. Let's read our survey results. "Round Table Survey Results. We asked forty children and teachers at the hall three questions. First, do you recycle more now than two years ago? Thirty people said yes. It seems that more people are recycling. Second, do you turn off the water while you brush your teeth? Twenty people said yes. That is half of the group. This might mean that many of us can still save more water. Third, do you always take only the food you can finish? Ten people said yes. That is a quarter. This might mean that many of us are still learning. We did not ask everyone in the world, so these results are only about our group." How many people said they recycle more? Thirty people. Why can't we say everyone? Because we only asked our group, and not everyone said yes. Now, phonics. A quarter means one of four equal parts. Fifty percent is the same as half. Seventy percent means seventy out of one hundred. Don't add an s to percent. About seventy percent of the Earth's surface is water. Next time, we'll put many points of view together in one big presentation. | Hello, everyone! | 1150 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, It seems that more people are recycling. Today, we give a big presentation to the whole Round Table. Amina, Priya, Nadia, Sam, Mateo and Liam sit with their teachers, ready to listen. Each of us holds a poster. Haru has the farmer, Riko has the shop, Birimo has the family, and Kiko has the sea. Ms. Emma teaches us two more linking words. Moreover adds another strong point. Therefore shows the result. Haru asks, Is moreover the same as and? Ms. Emma says, It's similar, but moreover sounds great in a presentation, and it adds a stronger point. Kiko practices the ending. Sharing food helps people. Moreover, it saves money. Therefore, we should share extra food. Say it with me. Moreover, it saves money. Therefore, we should share extra food. Brilliant! Now, let's read our presentation. "Today, we'd like to look at food from many points of view. From the farmer's point of view, every carrot is precious, even the funny-shaped ones. From the shop's point of view, it's hard to sell food that doesn't look perfect. However, wonky veggie boxes are helping. From a family's point of view, leftovers can become tasty new meals. From the sea's point of view, less plastic packaging means a cleaner home for turtles. When we share extra food with neighbors, fewer good things go to waste. Moreover, it saves money. Therefore, we should share extra food. Let's work together. Thank you for listening!" How many points of view did we talk about? Four: the farmer, the shop, the family and the sea. What does moreover do? It adds another strong point. The six children clap, and Orbit spins and spins. Every voice counts! Then the second lantern flickers and starts to glow with soft rainbow light. The Lantern of Many Views lit up! Two lanterns are shining now. Now, phonics. Let's review. One billion has nine zeros. One third means one of three equal parts. Renewable is re plus new plus able. Next time, Orbit will teach us about debates, where two teams take turns sharing different opinions. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, It seems that more people are recycling. | 1154 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Our first point is that gardens teach us about food. Today, it's the no team's turn. Kiko and Birimo listened carefully to the yes team. Now they must give a rebuttal. A rebuttal is an answer that shows a problem with the other team's idea. Orbit reminds everyone, Remember rule five. Disagree with ideas, not people. Kiko takes a deep breath. Gardens are lovely, but they need a lot of work. Who waters the plants every day? Who pulls the weeds? Birimo nods seriously. I'm playing a very tired gardener! He pretends to carry a heavy watering can and flops onto a cushion. Then Kiko says it clearly. We disagree because gardens take a lot of time. Say it with me. We disagree because gardens take a lot of time. Well done! Notice how Kiko starts. She doesn't say, You're wrong! She says, We disagree because, and then she gives a reason. Nadia helps the no team write their rebuttal card. Let's read it. "Rebuttal Card. The other team said that gardens teach us about food. That is a good point, and we agree that learning about food is important. However, we disagree because gardens take a lot of time. Plants need water every day, and weeds grow very fast. Teachers and students are already busy with lessons, clubs and tests. Also, what happens during the long summer holidays? Nobody is at school, so the plants might dry up. A garden needs a lot of water, too, and in some places water is precious. Instead of a big garden, students could visit a farm or grow herbs in small pots in the classroom. These ideas teach us about food, too, but they take much less time." What could happen during the summer holidays? The plants might dry up. What ideas does the no team give instead of a big garden? Visiting a farm or growing herbs in small pots. Haru scratches his head. Hmm, the summer holidays. That's a tough one. Riko smiles. Good point! Now we have to think harder. That's the magic of a debate. Now, phonics. When you disagree, stay calm, but say the key word strongly. We disaGREE because. We do NOT agree. The OTHer team said. Stress the big sound, then fall at the end. Next time, we'll have a real mini debate, with Orbit as our timer! | Hello, everyone! | 1155 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, We disagree because gardens take a lot of time. Today is the big day: our first mini debate! All six children and their teachers sit around the round table to watch. Orbit floats in the middle with a little clock on his front. I am the timer today! Each speaker has one minute. Birimo bows to the audience. I'm playing a famous debate star! Orbit chuckles and spins once. Let's begin! Riko goes first and gives the claim. Beep! The minute is up. Orbit says, Thank you. Now, the other team, please. Say it with me. Thank you. Now, the other team, please. Perfect! A good chair always thanks the speaker and gives the next team a turn. Let's read the debate script. "Orbit: Welcome to our mini debate! The topic is, Schools should have gardens. Yes team, please begin. Riko: We believe that schools should have gardens. Our first point is that gardens teach us about food. Orbit: Thank you. Now, the other team, please. Kiko: We disagree because gardens take a lot of time, especially during the summer holidays. Haru: That's a fair point. However, families and teachers can take turns, and we can plant vegetables that grow before summer. Birimo: The other team said families could help. But not every family has free time. Orbit: Thank you, both teams. Every voice counts! Now, let's thank each other." Who gave the yes team's claim? Riko. What did Haru say about the summer? Families and teachers can take turns, and they can plant vegetables that grow before summer. Then all four friends stand up and shake hands. Orbit asks for a show of hands, just for fun. Amina, Sam and Liam vote yes. Priya, Nadia and Mateo vote no. It's a tie! Everyone laughs. Orbit says, Both sides had good points, and both teams were kind. That is the real win! Suddenly, the third lantern by the table begins to shine. The Lantern of Debate lit up! Now three lanterns shine together. Now, phonics. Let's review. We beLIEVE that: stress believe. We disaGREE because: stress agree. Half means one of two equal parts, and we write it as one over two. Pollution comes from pollute: drop the e and add ion. Next time, we'll put our heads together and look for solutions. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, We disagree because gardens take a lot of time. Today is the big day: our first mini debate! All six children and their teachers sit around the round table to watch. Orbit floats in the middle with a little clock on his front. I am the timer today! Each speaker has one minute. Birimo bows to the audience. I'm playing a famous debate star! Orbit chuckles and spins once. Let's begin! Riko goes first and gives the claim. Beep! The minute is up. Orbit says, Thank you. Now, the other team, please. Say it with me. Thank you. Now, the other team, please. Perfect! A good chair always thanks the speaker and gives the next team a turn. Let's read the debate script. "Orbit: Welcome to our mini debate! The topic is, Schools should have gardens. Yes team, please begin. Riko: We believe that schools should have gardens. Our first point is that gardens teach us about food. Orbit: Thank you. Now, the other team, please. Kiko: We disagree because gardens take a lot of time, especially during summer vacation. Haru: That's a fair point. However, families and teachers can take turns, and we can plant vegetables that grow before summer. Birimo: The other team said families could help. But not every family has free time. Orbit: Thank you, both teams. Every voice counts! Now, let's thank each other." Who gave the yes team's claim? Riko. What did Haru say about the summer? Families and teachers can take turns, and they can plant vegetables that grow before summer. Then all four friends stand up and shake hands. Orbit asks for a show of hands, just for fun. Amina, Sam and Liam vote yes. Priya, Nadia and Mateo vote no. It's a tie! Everyone laughs. Orbit says, Both sides had good points, and both teams were kind. That is the real win! Suddenly, the third lantern by the table begins to shine. The Lantern of Debate lit up! Now three lanterns shine together. Now, phonics. Let's review. We beLIEVE that: stress believe. We disaGREE because: stress agree. Half means one of two equal parts, and we write it as one over two. Pollution comes from pollute: drop the e and add ion. Next time, we'll put our heads together and look for solutions. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, We disagree because gardens take a lot of time. Today is the big day: our first mini debate! | 1156 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Thank you. Now, the other team, please. The mini debate was fun, but today Orbit has a new challenge. We've talked about problems. Now, let's look for solutions! A solution is an idea that helps fix a problem. Orbit spins over the part of the map that says food waste. Remember, about one third of food is wasted. What can we do about it? Sam raises his hand first. In my town, there is a sharing fridge at the community center. People put extra food inside, and anyone who needs it can take some. Haru's eyes go wide. Anyone? For free? Sam nods. Yes. A bakery brings bread at the end of the day, and families bring vegetables from their gardens. Riko writes on the solution board. One solution is to share extra food. Say it with me. One solution is to share extra food. Fantastic! Kiko adds another idea. At school, we could have a sharing shelf. If you don't want your apple, you put it on the shelf, and someone else can enjoy it. Birimo raises both hands. I'm playing a hungry squirrel! I'll take all the nuts! Ms. Emma laughs. Then the shelf wouldn't be fair, Birimo. Let's read the notice on Sam's sharing fridge. "Welcome to the Sharing Fridge! This fridge belongs to everyone in our neighborhood. Please share extra food that is fresh and clean, such as fruit, vegetables, bread and sealed packages. Please write today's date on a sticker before you put food inside. However, please don't bring raw meat or fish, because they go bad quickly. Volunteers check the fridge every morning and every evening. Therefore, the food stays safe and the fridge stays clean. Take what you need, and leave some for others. For example, last week a bakery shared twenty loaves of bread, and every loaf found a home. Thank you for helping us waste less food!" Why shouldn't people bring raw meat or fish? Because they go bad quickly. How many loaves of bread did the bakery share? Twenty. Now, phonics. Linking words guide the listener. Say the first part strongly and take a short pause after the comma. HOWever, pause, shows a different idea. THEREfore, pause, shows a result. For exAMple, pause, gives an example. Next time, Nadia will show us how her whole town saves energy. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Thank you. Now, the other team, please. | 1158 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, If everyone turned off the lights, we would save energy. Today, Kiko is a little quiet. She looks at the big map with all its pins. There are so many problems in the world, she says softly. Food, water, energy... I'm just a kid. What can I do? Ms. Emma sits down next to her. Kiko, that's a very normal feeling. Here's something important for everyone. You don't have to fix the whole world. Small actions count. And if world news ever makes you feel worried, talk to a grown-up you trust, like a family member or a teacher. Kiko nods and smiles a little. Orbit floats down and spins gently. Think of raindrops. One raindrop is tiny, but many raindrops make a river. Small actions can make a big difference. Say it with me. Small actions can make a big difference. Beautiful! Liam shows everyone a chart from his class. Let's read Liam's chart. "Our Small Actions Chart. Here are six things kids can do every day. One: Bring your own water bottle. In addition, you can fill it at home or at school. Two: Turn off the tap while you brush your teeth. Three: Take only the food you can eat, and eat what you take. Four: Use both sides of the paper. Instead of throwing it away, use scrap paper for drawing. Five: Turn off the lights when you leave a room. Six: Ask a grown-up to help you sort your trash. In our class, twenty children did these things for one month. Each action was small, but together they added up. In conclusion, you don't need to be a superhero. You just need to start." How many children did the actions? Twenty. What can you do while you brush your teeth? Turn off the tap. Birimo puts a towel on his back like a cape. I'm playing Captain Tap! Drip, drip, no more drips! Everyone laughs, even Kiko. Then Kiko picks one action for herself. I'll use scrap paper for my drawings. Now, phonics. In adDItion adds one more idea. InSTEAD means in place of that. In conCLUsion ends your talk. Stress the strong part and pause after the comma. Next time, we'll put everyone's ideas together. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, If everyone turned off the lights, we would save energy. Today, Kiko is a little quiet. She looks at the big map with all its pins. There are so many problems in the world, she says softly. Food, water, energy... I'm just a kid. What can I do? I sit down next to her. Kiko, that's a very normal feeling. Here's something important for everyone. You don't have to fix the whole world. Small actions count. And if world news ever makes you feel worried, talk to a grown-up you trust, like a family member or a teacher. Kiko nods and smiles a little. Orbit floats down and spins gently. Think of raindrops. One raindrop is tiny, but many raindrops make a river. Small actions can make a big difference. Say it with me. Small actions can make a big difference. Beautiful! Liam shows everyone a chart from his class. Let's read Liam's chart. "Our Small Actions Chart. Here are six things kids can do every day. One: Bring your own water bottle. In addition, you can fill it at home or at school. Two: Turn off the tap while you brush your teeth. Three: Take only the food you can eat, and eat what you take. Four: Use both sides of the paper. Instead of throwing it away, use scrap paper for drawing. Five: Turn off the lights when you leave a room. Six: Ask a grown-up to help you sort your trash. In our class, twenty children did these things for one month. Each action was small, but together they added up. In conclusion, you don't need to be a superhero. You just need to start." How many children did the actions? Twenty. What can you do while you brush your teeth? Turn off the tap. Birimo puts a towel on his back like a cape. I'm playing Captain Tap! Drip, drip, no more drips! Everyone laughs, even Kiko. Then Kiko picks one action for herself. I'll use scrap paper for my drawings. Now, phonics. In adDItion adds one more idea. InSTEAD means in place of that. In conCLUsion ends your talk. Stress the strong part and pause after the comma. Next time, we'll put everyone's ideas together. | A+C (block rule) | Ms. Emma reads her own script, so her own narration "Ms. Emma says / asks / smiles / gives ..." -> "I say / I ask / I smile / I give ..." (same as W17-38, say + subtitles + teacherScript); JA エマ先生 -> わたし. Children talking TO Ms. Emma, and cards / letters / speeches the children wrote (and questions about them), stay |

| 1160 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Let's combine our ideas. Today, the friends turn their ideas into an action plan. An action plan tells you what to do, who will do it and when. Orbit says, A good plan has steps, like stairs. You climb one step at a time. Haru, Riko, Kiko and Birimo work with Sam and Priya. They write each step on a big card. Then Riko stands in front of the round table and says, Our action plan has three steps. Say it with me. Our action plan has three steps. Wonderful! Let's read the action plan. "Our Action Plan: The Sharing Fridge Project. Our goal: to waste less food in our neighborhood. Step one: Next week, we will ask the grown-ups at the community center if we can use an old fridge. Ms. Emma will help us write a polite letter. Step two: After that, we will make posters and practice Kiko's song. However, we will only put up posters where the community center says it's okay. Step three: At the end of the month, we will open the sharing fridge with our families and teachers. Grown-ups will check the fridge every day. We hope that fifty percent of the extra bread from the bakery will go into the fridge instead of the trash. Therefore, everyone can help, step by step." Who will help write the letter? Ms. Emma. Who will check the fridge every day? Grown-ups. Amina raises her hand. What happens if nobody uses the fridge? Haru thinks. Good question. Then we'll sing Kiko's song louder! Everyone laughs, and Riko adds, And we'll ask people why, and change our plan. Amina smiles. That's a smart answer. Orbit spins so fast that he sparkles. Every voice counts! At that moment, the fourth lantern begins to shine. The Lantern of Action lit up! Now four lanterns shine around the table. Now, phonics. Let's review. HOWever, pause, shows a different idea. THEREfore, pause, shows a result. Our FIRST point is: stress first. Fifty percent is the same as half, and there is no s on percent. Next time, we'll start preparing for the Children's World Conference, and each team will practice with a timer. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Let's combine our ideas. | 1161 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Our action plan has three steps. Our sharing fridge plan is ready, and for the conference we will share a bigger idea for every school. Now the Children's World Conference is only one week away! This morning, Orbit floats above the round table and spins slowly. He says, Every voice counts! But every voice also needs a little time. So here is the rule for the conference. Each team will present for two minutes. Say it with me. Each team will present for two minutes. Great! Two minutes isn't very long, so we need to practice. Haru looks worried. Two minutes? I could talk about the sharing shelf for two hours! Birimo laughs and says, I'm playing a stopwatch! Tick, tock, tick, tock! Ms. Emma gives everyone a pile of small cue cards. Let's read Orbit's tips for the conference. "Getting Ready for the Children's World Conference. Each team will present for two minutes, so plan carefully. First, choose your most important idea. Don't try to say everything. Second, write only key words on your cue cards, not whole sentences. Then you can look up and smile at the audience. Third, decide who will speak first, second and last. Everyone on the team should have a part. Fourth, practice with a stopwatch. If you go over two minutes, cut something out. Finally, remember that it's OK to feel nervous. Take a deep breath, speak slowly, and look at a friendly face. The other children want to hear your ideas, and they will be on your side. Every voice counts!" What should you write on your cue cards? Only key words. What can you do if you feel nervous? Take a deep breath, speak slowly and look at a friendly face. In our first rehearsal, we talk for three minutes and twenty seconds. Oops! In our second rehearsal, Haru cuts his long story about the lunch tray, and we finish in one minute and fifty seconds. Perfect! Now, phonics. These words help us be careful when we are not one hundred percent sure. Seem means looks like. The audience seems friendly. Might means maybe. We might finish early. Probably means about eighty percent sure. We will probably feel nervous, and that's OK. Next time, the conference begins, and Kiko will give the opening speech. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Our action plan has three steps. Our sharing fridge plan is ready, and for the conference we will share a bigger idea for every school. Third, decide who will speak first, second and last. Everyone on the team should have a part. Fourth, practice with a stopwatch. If you go over two minutes, cut something out. Finally, remember that it's OK to feel nervous. Take a deep breath, speak slowly, and look at a friendly face. The other children want to hear your ideas, and they will be on your side. Every voice counts!" What should you write on your cue cards? Only key words. What can you do if you feel nervous? Take a deep breath, speak slowly and look at a friendly face. In our first rehearsal, we talk for three minutes and twenty seconds. Oops! In our second rehearsal, Haru cuts his long story about the lunch tray, and we finish in one minute and fifty seconds. Perfect! Now, phonics. These words help us be careful when we are not one hundred percent sure. Seem means looks like. The audience seems friendly. Might means maybe. We might finish early. Probably means about eighty percent sure. We will probably feel nervous, and that's OK. Next time, the conference begins, and Kiko will give the opening speech. | Hello, everyone! | 1164 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, We propose that every school has a sharing shelf. This will help reduce food waste. After our presentation, Orbit rings his bell. Question time! Hands go up all around the round table. Amina raises her hand first. She says our idea is wonderful, and then she adds, That's a great point. However, someone needs to look after the shelf. Say it with me. That's a great point. However, someone needs to look after the shelf. Great! When you like part of an idea but have a worry, you can say, That's a great point. However, and then share your worry kindly. Riko answers, Thank you, Amina. Students can take turns as shelf helpers each week, with a teacher. Then Mateo asks what happens to the food on Friday afternoon, and Nadia asks how schools will keep the food safe. Ms. Emma writes everything down. Let's read the question and answer notes. "Questions and Answers. Question one, from Amina: Who will look after the shelf? Answer: Students will take turns as shelf helpers each week, with a teacher. Helpers check the shelf at lunch and keep it tidy. Question two, from Mateo: What happens to food that is still on the shelf on Friday? Answer: On Friday afternoon, the helpers and the teacher can give it to the school kitchen or to families who would like it. That way, almost nothing is thrown away. Question three, from Nadia: How will we keep the food safe? Answer: Only fruit and closed snacks can go on the shelf, and the helpers check the dates. Generally, these foods stay fresh for a few days. Thank you for your great questions!" Who will look after the shelf? Students, as shelf helpers each week, with a teacher. What food can go on the shelf? Only fruit and closed snacks. Birimo whispers, I'm playing a shelf helper! I'll guard the bananas day and night! Everyone laughs. Our plan is better now, thanks to the questions. Now, phonics. Generally means in most cases. Generally, fruit stays fresh for a few days. Almost means nearly, but not one hundred percent. Almost nothing is thrown away. About means around a number. About one third of food is wasted. Next time, the delegates will try to find one idea that everyone can accept. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, We propose that every school has a sharing shelf. This will help reduce food waste. After our presentation, Orbit rings his bell. Question time! | 1166 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, We all agree that no food should go to waste. The first part of the conference is over, and five lanterns are shining around the round table. Today, Orbit gives us a new challenge: reading. He says, Before you give your opinion, read more than one side. Nadia has brought two short articles from her town's newspaper. Her town is thinking about turning off some street lights at night. Riko reads them and says, Article A and article B have different opinions. Say it with me. Article A and article B have different opinions. Great! Let's read the two articles. "Article A: Let the Night Be Dark. Our town has hundreds of street lights, and many of them shine all night on empty roads. Our main point is simple: we should turn some of them off after midnight. This would save energy and money. In addition, we could see the stars again. Night animals such as owls, moths and bats need darkness to find food and their way home. Article B: Keep Our Streets Bright. Some people walk home late from work, and bright streets help them feel safe. Lights also make it easier to see steps, bikes and cars. Older people may find dark streets difficult. It might seem like a small change, but everyone should feel comfortable outside at night. We believe that street lights are important for everyone. Both writers care about our town. Which article do you agree with?" What are the reasons in article A? To save energy and money, to see the stars, and to help night animals. Why does article B want to keep the lights on? People feel safe, and it's easier to see. Haru says, Article A is right! Kiko says, Wait, article B makes a good point, too. Ms. Emma smiles. That's exactly why we read both sides. Now, phonics. Let's review. Sustainable is sustain plus able: it can keep going. Ten million has seven zeros. Our main point is: say MAIN strongly. In addition adds another point, so pause after the comma. Seem means look like. Next time, we'll write an opinion essay that mentions both sides. | Hello, everyone! | 1167 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Article A and article B have different opinions. Today, Orbit asks each of us to write a short opinion essay about the street lights. He says a good essay is like a fair judge. It shows both sides, and then it gives your opinion with reasons. Riko starts her essay like this: Some people think street lights should stay on all night. However, I believe we can turn some off. Say it with me. Some people think street lights should stay on all night. However, I believe we can turn some off. Excellent! First, you show the other side. Then you say what you believe. Let's read Riko's essay. "Street Lights at Night. Some people think street lights should stay on all night. However, I believe we can turn some off. On the one hand, lights help people feel safe when they walk home, and they make it easier to see. This is an important point, and we should listen to it. On the other hand, many lights shine on empty parks and fields where nobody walks at night. These lights use a lot of energy, and they make it hard for owls and bats to find food. Therefore, I believe we should turn off the lights in empty places after midnight and keep the lights on busy streets. Perhaps we could also use sensor lights that turn on when someone walks by. In this way, people can stay safe, and night animals can enjoy the dark. We can take care of both people and nature." Which lights does Riko want to keep on? The lights on busy streets. What idea does Riko add near the end? Sensor lights that turn on when someone walks by. Kiko writes the opposite opinion, and her essay is fair and kind, too. Haru asks, Wait, really? We can have different opinions and still both be fair? Ms. Emma nods. Yes, of course! Now, phonics. Let's review. Conservation is conserve, drop the e, plus ation. Two thirds has an s, because there are two. We strongly believe: say STRONG strongly. On the other hand shows the other side. Perhaps means maybe. Next time, we'll write a message to the whole world. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Article A and article B have different opinions. | 1167 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Article A and article B have different opinions. Today, Orbit asks each of us to write a short opinion essay about the street lights. He says a good essay is like a fair judge. It shows both sides, and then it gives your opinion with reasons. Riko starts her essay like this: Some people think street lights should stay on all night. However, I believe we can turn some off. Say it with me. Some people think street lights should stay on all night. However, I believe we can turn some off. Excellent! First, you show the other side. Then you say what you believe. Let's read Riko's essay. "Street Lights at Night. Some people think street lights should stay on all night. However, I believe we can turn some off. On the one hand, lights help people feel safe when they walk home, and they make it easier to see. This is an important point, and we should listen to it. On the other hand, many lights shine on empty parks and fields where nobody walks at night. These lights use a lot of energy, and they make it hard for owls and bats to find food. Therefore, I believe we should turn off the lights in empty places after midnight and keep the lights on busy streets. Perhaps we could also use sensor lights that turn on when someone walks by. In this way, people can stay safe, and night animals can enjoy the dark. We can take care of both people and nature." Which lights does Riko want to keep on? The lights on busy streets. What idea does Riko add near the end? Sensor lights that turn on when someone walks by. Kiko writes the opposite opinion, and her essay is fair and kind, too. Haru asks, Wait, really? We can have different opinions and still both be fair? I nod. Yes, of course! Now, phonics. Let's review. Conservation is conserve, drop the e, plus ation. Two thirds has an s, because there are two. We strongly believe: say STRONG strongly. On the other hand shows the other side. Perhaps means maybe. Next time, we'll write a message to the whole world. | Hello, everyone! | 1168 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Some people think street lights should stay on all night. However, I believe we can turn some off. Today, Orbit has a special idea. He says, Let's send a message from the Round Table to the whole world! All the children sit together. Priya suggests starting with Dear world. Sam wants to write about food, Amina wants to write about water, and Liam wants to write about the sea. Mateo says, Don't forget the farmers! Nadia adds, And the stars! Birimo wants a line about pancakes, but everyone laughs and says, Maybe next time. Then Kiko writes the first line: Dear world, we children want a cleaner and kinder planet. Say it with me. Dear world, we children want a cleaner and kinder planet. Lovely! Let's read our message. "Dear world, we children want a cleaner and kinder planet. About eight billion people live on Earth, and we all share one home. We came to the Round Table from every continent. We learned that big problems have many sides, and that listening is the first step. Let me explain why we are hopeful. People everywhere are already helping. Towns are building new wells, buses are bringing books to villages, and classes are cleaning beaches with their teachers. We will do our part, too. We will take only what we can eat, turn off lights we don't need, and share kindly. Please work with us. If everyone helps a little, our planet will likely become a better home for all of us. With love, the children of the Round Table." About how many people live on Earth? About eight billion. What will the children do? Take only what they can eat, turn off lights they don't need, and share kindly. Orbit rolls up the message and places it in the center of the round table. As a result, the whole table seems to hum softly. Now, phonics. Let's review. Global is globe, drop the e, plus al. Eight billion has nine zeros after the eight, and no s on billion. Let me explain why: say WHY strongly. As a result shows a result, so pause after the comma. Likely means it will probably happen. Next time, we'll say goodbye to our new friends around the hall. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Some people think street lights should stay on all night. | 1169 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Dear world, we children want a cleaner and kinder planet. Tomorrow is the closing ceremony, so today we take a goodbye walk around the Hall of the Round Table. We stop at our world map of challenges, which still has all our pins and cards. We remember Sam's lunch talk about food waste, Amina's new well, and Priya's library bus. We visit Mateo, whose uncle grows funny-shaped carrots, and Liam, who cleaned his beach with his class. We laugh about our garden debate, when Kiko and Birimo said gardens take a lot of time. Then we thank each of the six children. Haru says, You taught us to see things from many sides. Riko adds, We believe that every point of view matters. Say it with me. We believe that every point of view matters. Wonderful! Let's read our thank-you card. "Dear Amina, Priya, Nadia, Sam, Mateo and Liam, thank you for sharing your stories with us. Amina, your well showed us that clean water changes lives. Priya, your library bus showed us that books can travel. Nadia, you taught us to think about lights and stars. Sam, you helped us understand shops. Mateo, you made us love funny-shaped carrots. Liam, you showed us how to care for the sea. Before we came here, we usually thought there was only one right answer. Now we believe that every point of view matters. We will never forget you, and we will keep your stories in our hearts. Your friends, Haru, Riko, Kiko and Birimo." What did Priya's library bus show the friends? That books can travel. What did the friends usually think before they came? That there was only one right answer. The six children give us big smiles and warm hugs, and Orbit spins slowly, humming a happy tune. Now, phonics. Let's review. Endangered is en plus danger plus ed: it means in danger. A quarter is one out of four. The other team said: say OTHer strongly. In conclusion starts the end of your talk, so pause after the comma. Usually means most of the time. Next time, it's the grand closing ceremony of the Children's World Conference! | Hello, everyone! | 1170 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, We believe that every point of view matters. Today is the closing ceremony of the Children's World Conference! The hall is full of families, teachers and children. Orbit floats above the round table, and five lanterns are shining around it. Only the sixth one is still dark. Orbit asks each team to share one hope for the future. Amina hopes every town will have clean water nearby. Priya hopes every child can go to school. Sam hopes no food will go to waste. Then Orbit asks everyone to stand, hold hands, and say it together. Together, we can make the world a better place. Say it with me. Together, we can make the world a better place. Fantastic! Let's read Orbit's closing words. "Dear children of the Round Table, four weeks ago, you came here with different languages, different homes and different ideas. | 1171 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Together, we can make the world a better place. Today, the beam of light from the Round Table turns into a stairway of light. Each step glows softly and rings like a tiny bell when we step on it. We climb up and up, through the clouds, holding hands. Birimo hums a happy song the whole way. At the top, we find a quiet garden of light. The grass is soft and silver, little lights float in the air like fireflies, and in the middle there is a calm pool of starlight. Then a tiny glowing star-creature flies toward us. Her voice sounds like a soft bell. Welcome, friends. I'm Celeste, the keeper of this star. Shine on! She tells us that this is the Star of Beginnings. Haru remembers Orbit's words and says them again. This star shows where every journey began. Say it with me. This star shows where every journey began. Wonderful! Celeste points to a silver sign in the grass. Let's read Celeste's welcome words. "Welcome to the Star of Beginnings. Every journey with words begins with a small light. Maybe it is the first time you say hello, or the first word you can read. This star keeps all of those little lights, and today it keeps yours, too. Look up. The star has six points, or rays. Right now, all six rays are dim. As you look back on your adventures and get ready for what comes next, something wonderful will happen. Take your time here, my little lights. Rest on the soft silver grass, look into the pool, and remember how far you have come. Shine on!" What does every journey with words begin with? A small light. How many rays does the star have? Six, and right now they are all dim. Celeste flies around Birimo once and gives him a little smile, as if she already knows him. Birimo blinks. Have we met before? Celeste only giggles. Now, phonics. Let's look back at some spelling patterns from our first years. Rain has a and i in the middle. Play has a and y at the end. Dream has e and a in the middle. They all make long sounds, and each spelling has its own place. Next time, the pool of starlight will show us a very special memory. | Hello, everyone! | 1172 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, This star shows where every journey began. Today, Celeste leads us to the calm pool of starlight. Look into the pool, she says softly. It remembers everything. At first, we only see our own faces. Then the water ripples, and a picture appears. It's a green forest with tall trees and a little path. It's the Forest of Beginnings! We see a small boy and a small girl standing under a tree. They look so young! Haru laughs and points at the pool. That's me! And that's you, Riko! Then Haru turns to all of us. Do you remember our first lesson? We said, Hello! I'm Haru. Say it with me. Do you remember our first lesson? We said, Hello! I'm Haru. Lovely! A memory page floats up from the water, shining like the moon. Let's read the memory page. "Memory Page: The Forest of Beginnings. On the very first day, the forest was green and quiet. A boy waved and said, Hello! I'm Haru. A girl smiled and said, Hi! I'm Riko. Nice to meet you. Then Ms. Emma taught four magic words with actions: Look! Stop! Come here! Go! Everybody pointed, froze, waved and ran. On the second day, a girl named Kiko waved and said, Bye-bye, Haru! A few days later, a round, fluffy friend yawned and said, I'm sleepy. But when the children played with him, he jumped up and shouted, Yes! I'm happy now! That fluffy friend was Birimo." What were the four magic words? Look! Stop! Come here! Go! How did Birimo feel at first? He was sleepy. Everyone looks at Birimo, and we all laugh. Birimo covers his face with his paws. I was very young then! Celeste smiles at him again, and her light twinkles. Ms. Emma smiles warmly. You have all grown so much, she says. Now, phonics. Let's review three more spelling patterns. Green has two e's in the middle. Boat has o and a in the middle. Snow has o and w at the end. Boat and snow have the same sound, but oa goes in the middle and ow goes at the end. Next time, we'll talk about everything we can do now. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, This star shows where every journey began. Today, Celeste leads us to the calm pool of starlight. | 1173 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Do you remember our first lesson? We said, Hello! I'm Haru. Today, we sit on the silver grass next to the pool of starlight. Celeste floats down and asks a gentle question. Little lights, what can you do now that you couldn't do before? We all start talking at once! Riko can write her own stories. Haru can explain how deep-sea fish make light. Kiko can talk about feelings and listen kindly. Birimo can order pancakes in five different ways. Ms. Emma laughs and gives each of us a piece of glowing paper. Let's make a can-do list! Kiko looks at all the papers and says, I've learned so much since I started. Say it with me. I've learned so much since I started. Super! Kiko reads her can-do list first. Let's read Kiko's can-do list. "My Can-Do List. Six years ago, I could only say hello and bye-bye. I've learned so much since I started! Now I can read whole books in English, and I can tell my own stories. I can give a short presentation and answer questions about it. I can share my opinion and listen to a different opinion kindly. I can talk about my feelings and help a friend who feels sad. I can read a map, ask for directions and order food. I've made friends from many places, and I've met amazing guides like Ember, Skye and Orbit. I still make mistakes, but now I know that mistakes help me learn." What could Kiko say six years ago? | 1175 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, My favorite world was the Labyrinth of Tales because I loved writing stories. Today, we make a long timeline on the silver grass. Ms. Emma gives us six glowing ribbons, one for each year of our journey. We write the most important memories on each ribbon and lay them in a line. Then we stand at the start of the timeline, and Celeste floats in front of us, ready to listen. Haru and Riko present the first part together. In the first year, we learned to say hello. By the third year, we could tell stories. Say it with me. In the first year, we learned to say hello. By the third year, we could tell stories. Excellent! Then each of us reads one part of the timeline. Let's read our timeline. "Our Journey Timeline. Year One: In the Forest of Beginnings, we learned to say hello, count and name colors. Year Two: In the Kingdom of English, Birimo found a glowing map of worlds that were not on any map yet. Year Three: In the Ruins of Memory, we read our first real stories. Year Four: We made video calls, read books in the Amber Library and acted in plays. Year Five: We shared opinions at the Lighthouse of Ideas and designed inventions in the City of Tomorrow. Year Six: We gave presentations, wrote our own stories, learned about feelings and joined a children's conference. Every year, we grew a little more, together." What did Birimo find in Year Two? A glowing map. Where did the friends read their first real stories? In the Ruins of Memory. When we finish, the star begins to hum softly. One of the six rays starts to glow, brighter and brighter. The Ray of Memories lit up! Celeste claps her tiny hands. You remembered every step. Shine on! Birimo counts on his paws. One down, five to go! Now, phonics. Let's review. Rain has ai in the middle. Snow has ow at the end. Phone has ph, which sounds like f. Thumb has a silent b at the end. Next time, we'll open our portfolios and choose our best work. | Hello, everyone! | 1176 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, In the first year, we learned to say hello. By the third year, we could tell stories. Today, Celeste gives each of us a shining folder. Inside is our portfolio（作品集）, a collection of our best work from six years. There are stories, speeches, letters, posters and even recordings of our voices. Riko opens her folder and gasps. My first story is in here! It's only three lines long! We all preview our folders and laugh at our early drawings. Then Ms. Emma asks us to choose one piece of work that makes us really proud. Riko chooses right away. She holds up a thick stack of pages with a golden cover. This is the story I wrote in World 37. Say it with me. This is the story I wrote in World 37. Wonderful! Riko reads the label she wrote for it. Let's read Riko's portfolio label. "My Best Work: The Moon Fox. This is the story I wrote in World 37, in the Labyrinth of Tales. It's about a little fox who wants to touch the moon. At first, the fox is unhappy because the moon is too far away. Then she learns that the moon's light touches her softly every night. I chose this story because I worked on it for a long time. I had to rewrite the ending three times! Nib the mouse gave me great ideas, and my friends helped me make the middle more exciting. When I read it on storytelling night, everyone clapped. It shows that I can write a whole story in English." How many times did Riko rewrite the ending? Three times. Why did she choose this story? Because she worked on it for a long time. Haru chooses his deep-sea presentation from World 36. Kiko chooses her opening speech from the Round Table. And Birimo proudly holds up a pancake recipe card from the market. It's a classic! he says. Now, phonics. Unhappy is un plus happy, so it means not happy. Rewrite is re plus write, so it means write again. Preview is pre plus view, so it means look before. Next time, we'll compare the old us and the new us. | Hello, everyone! | 1177 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, This is the story I wrote in World 37. Today, Ms. Emma draws a big chart on a sheet of light. On the left side, it says Me Then. On the right side, it says Me Now. Celeste explains that the pool of starlight can show both. When Kiko looks into the water, she sees a tiny Kiko hiding behind a tree in the Forest of Beginnings. Then the water ripples, and she sees herself at the Round Table, giving the opening speech in front of children from every continent. Kiko smiles and says, At first, I could only say hello. Now, I can give a speech in English. Say it with me. At first, I could only say hello. Now, I can give a speech in English. Fantastic! Kiko fills in her chart and reads it to us. Let's read Kiko's chart. "Me Then, Me Now by Kiko. Me then: I was very shy. I used to hide when someone talked to me. I could only say hello and bye-bye, and I didn't like reading aloud. Me now: I enjoy meeting new friends, and I can ask them questions. I can read long stories aloud with feeling. At first, I could only say hello. Now, I can give a speech in English! I used to be careless with spelling, but now I check my writing carefully. I still feel nervous sometimes, but I have learned to take a deep breath and smile. The biggest change is this: I believe in myself now." What did Kiko use to do when someone talked to her? She used to hide. What is Kiko's biggest change? She believes in herself now. Haru's chart says he used to think English was too hard, but now he reads science books for fun. Riko used to write only three lines, but now she writes whole stories. Birimo used to fall asleep in lessons. And now? He only falls asleep after lunch. Now, phonics. Dislike is dis plus like, so it means the opposite of like. Careful is care plus ful, so it means full of care. Careless is care plus less, so it means without care. Careful and careless are opposites. Next time, we'll talk about the hardest parts of our journey. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, This is the story I wrote in World 37. Today, I draw a big chart on a sheet of light. | 1178 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, At first, I could only say hello. Now, I can give a speech in English. Today, Celeste asks a brave question. Little lights, what was the hardest part of your journey? Everyone is quiet for a moment. Then Haru laughs. Past tense! I used to say goed and eated. Riko says R and L were hard for her, because right and light sounded the same at first. Birimo says the hardest part was waiting for his turn. Everyone giggles. Then Kiko takes a deep breath. The hardest part was speaking in front of people, but I kept going. Say it with me. The hardest part was speaking in front of people, but I kept going. Brilliant! Kiko shows us a page from her diary. Let's read Kiko's hard-parts diary. "My Hard-Parts Diary. In the third year, I had to read a short poem in front of the class. My hands were shaking, and my voice was very small. I read too quickly, and I made two mistakes. That night, I felt sad. But Ms. Emma said, Being nervous means you care. My friends practiced with me every day. Riko clapped, Haru gave me thumbs-ups, and Birimo made funny faces so I would relax. Little by little, my voice got stronger. The hardest part was speaking in front of people, but I kept going. Last month, I gave the opening speech at the Round Table. My friends' kindness helped me so much." Why did Kiko feel sad that night? Because she read too quickly and made two mistakes. What did Birimo do to help? He made funny faces so she would relax. Celeste shines warmly. Hard parts are part of every journey. Shine on! Ms. Emma adds, Every one of you kept going, and that is what matters most. Now, phonics. Kindness is kind plus ness, and ness makes a noun. Quickly is quick plus ly, and ly tells us how. Movement is move plus ment, and ment makes a noun, too. Next time, we'll make cards for children who are just starting to learn English. | Hello, everyone! | 1179 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, The hardest part was speaking in front of people, but I kept going. Today, the pool of starlight shows something new. We see the Forest of Beginnings again, but this time, we are not in the picture. Instead, we see a group of new first-year children standing under the trees with their teacher. One little boy waves and says, Hello! Everyone gasps. They're just starting, like we did! Celeste asks us to write advice cards for them. Riko thinks for a moment and writes the first card. My advice is to listen to English every day. Say it with me. My advice is to listen to English every day. Perfect! Then we all write our cards together. Let's read our advice cards. "Advice Cards for New Learners. From Riko: My advice is to listen to English every day. Songs, stories and cartoons all help. From Haru: Don't be afraid of mistakes. I made so many mistakes, and they taught me a lot. From Kiko: Read aloud, even if you feel shy. Your voice will get stronger, little by little. From Birimo: Sing songs! Singing makes English feel comfortable, like a warm pancake. From all of us: Learning a new language may seem impossible at first, but it isn't. Take one small step every day. Be kind to yourself, and ask your friends and teachers for help. You can do it! We believe in you." What does Birimo say singing does? It makes English feel comfortable. What does Haru say about mistakes? Don't be afraid of them, because they teach you a lot. Celeste gathers the cards. Each card turns into a tiny light, and the lights float down, down, down to the forest. The new children look up and smile. Now, phonics. Invention is invent plus ion, and ion makes a noun. Comfortable is comfort plus able, so it means nice and cozy. Impossible is im plus possible, so it means not possible. Before p, we use im, not in. Next time, we'll show our portfolios to Celeste and Ms. Emma. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, The hardest part was speaking in front of people, but I kept going. Invention is invent plus ion, and ion makes a noun. Comfortable is comfort plus able, so it means nice and cozy. Impossible is im plus possible, so it means not possible. Before p, we use im, not in. Next time, we'll show our portfolios to Celeste and me. | A+C (block rule) | Ms. Emma reads her own script, so her own narration "Ms. Emma says / asks / smiles / gives ..." -> "I say / I ask / I smile / I give ..." | 1180 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, My advice is to listen to English every day. Today is our portfolio showcase. Celeste and Ms. Emma sit on the silver grass, and the floating lights gather around us to listen. Each of us has five minutes to present our best work. Haru goes first. He stands up straight, takes a deep breath, smiles and opens his folder. Let me show you my portfolio. Say it with me. Let me show you my portfolio. Excellent! Haru begins with the very first page. Let's read Haru's portfolio introduction. "Let me show you my portfolio. On the first page, there is a drawing of a fish from Year One. Under it, I wrote one word: fish. On the next pages, you can see how my writing grew. In Year Three, I wrote short diaries. In Year Five, I made a poster about an invention that cleans the sea. My best work is my deep-sea presentation from World 36. It explains how some deep-sea fish make their own light. I was unhappy with my first draft because it was too long, so I made it shorter and clearer. I practiced my hand movements and spoke slowly. This shows that I can explain science in English. My dream is to keep learning about the ocean. Do you have any questions?" What did Haru write under his first drawing? One word: fish. Why was Haru unhappy with his first draft? Because it was too long. Then Riko, Kiko and Birimo present, too. Celeste asks careful questions, and everyone answers clearly. Birimo even explains how his recipe card shows his growth: his first pancake was flat, but his last one was fluffy! When the last folder closes, the star hums, and the second ray begins to glow. The Ray of Growth lit up! Celeste rings like a bell. You have grown so much. Shine on! Now, phonics. Let's review. Unhappy is un plus happy. Careful is care plus ful. Movement is move plus ment. Dream has ea in the middle. Next time, we'll start practicing for an interview test, and the first question will be all about you. | Hello, everyone! | 1181 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Let me show you my portfolio. Two rays of the star are shining now. Today, Celeste has a new idea. She says, Many learners take a speaking test at the end of a long trip with words. It isn't scary. It's a chance to show how much you've grown. Celeste makes a little room of light with a table and two chairs. Ms. Emma sits behind the table. She will be the examiner. Birimo jumps into the other chair and says, I'm playing the examiner, too! You can have a turn later, Birimo. First, we practice how an interview begins. You knock on the door, walk in, and say good morning. Then you wait until the examiner says, Please sit down. The first question is often the same. Please tell me about yourself. Say it with me. Please tell me about yourself. Wonderful! In our practice, you answer as your story self. You don't need to say your real name or your school. A nickname is fine. Let's read the interview guide. "Interview Guide. Step one: Knock on the door, walk in, and say good morning or hello. Step two: Sit down when the examiner asks you to. Sit up straight and smile. Step three: Listen carefully. When the examiner says, Please tell me about yourself, talk about the things you like. You can talk about your hobbies, your favorite food, or something fun you do on weekends. Try to say three or four sentences, and add a reason or an example. Step four: When the examiner says thank you, say thank you, too, and walk out calmly. Don't worry about small mistakes. Examiners want to hear your ideas, not perfect English." What should you do when you sit down? Sit up straight and smile. How many sentences should you try to say? Three or four. Haru goes first. He says, I love swimming. I go to the pool every weekend, and I also like reading about sea animals. Ms. Emma nods. That was clear and friendly. Celeste looks at Birimo with a little smile, as if she knows something about him. Now, phonics. Some long words share a root. Inspect has spect, which means look, like spectator. Construct has struct, which means build, like structure. Predict has dict, which means say, like dictionary. Next time, we'll talk about a picture in an interview. | Hello, everyone! | 1181 | `teacher.segments[0].subtitleJa` | みんな、こんにちは！ まえの かいは「わたしの ポートフォリオを 見せるね」と いったね。星の ひかりの すじは いま 2 本 かがやいて いるよ。きょうは セレステが、めんせつの ための ひかりの 小さな へやを つくって くれるよ！ | みんな、こんにちは！ まえの かいは「わたしの ポートフォリオを 見せるね」と いったね。星の ひかりの すじは いま 2 本 かがやいて いるよ。きょうは セレストが、めんせつの ための ひかりの 小さな へやを つくって くれるよ！ | objective (JA) | Celeste is セレスト everywhere else in W40 (78 times, and the test name); セレステ is a variant |
| 1182 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Please tell me about yourself. All four of us practiced our self-introductions, and even Birimo answered without talking about pancakes for a whole minute. Today, Ms. Emma brings out a picture card. In many interview tests, the examiner shows you a picture and asks you about it. You don't need to tell a story. You just say what you can see, clearly and calmly. Ms. Emma holds up the card. It shows a sunny park. She points to the boy and asks the question. Look at the picture. What is the boy doing? Say it with me. Look at the picture. What is the boy doing? Great! The answer uses the ing form, because it's happening in the picture right now. He's walking a dog. Let's read the model answer for the picture card. "In this picture, I can see a sunny park. There are tall trees and a small pond. A boy is walking a dog. The dog has a red leash, and it looks very happy. Under a big tree, a girl is reading a book. Maybe it's an exciting story, because she's smiling. In the middle of the park, two children are flying a kite. The kite is shaped like a fish, and it's very high in the sky. Near the gate, a man is selling ice cream from a little cart. There are three people waiting in line. I think it's a hot day, because everyone wants ice cream." What is the girl doing? She's reading a book under a tree. How many people are waiting for ice cream? Three. Ms. Emma explains a useful trick. First, say the big picture, like There are tall trees. Then talk about each person with is and ing. Finally, add one small idea of your own, like I think it's a hot day. Then Birimo grabs the picture card. I'm playing the examiner! He asks Haru about the boy, Kiko about the kite, and then he stops and stares at the ice cream cart for a very long time. Celeste giggles like a little bell. Now, phonics. Erupt has rupt, which means break, like interrupt. Project has ject, which means throw, like reject. Transport has port, which means carry, like export. Next time, we'll give our opinions and our reasons. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Please tell me about yourself. | 1183 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Look at the picture. What is the boy doing? We described the park, and Birimo finally got his ice cream, at least in his imagination. Today, we practice the hardest part of the interview: giving an opinion. Ms. Emma shows us an opinion card with one question on it. Do you think more people will ride bikes in the future? Why? Say it with me. Do you think more people will ride bikes in the future? Why? Great job! Kiko looks worried. What if I give the wrong answer? Ms. Emma smiles. In an opinion question, there is no wrong answer. You can say I think so, or I don't think so. The examiner wants to hear what you think and why. Try to give two reasons. Let's read the opinion card. "Opinion Card. Question: Do you think more people will ride bikes in the future? Why? Answer A: I think so, for two reasons. First, riding a bike is good for your health. Second, bikes don't make the air dirty, so more people will choose them to keep the air fresh. Answer B: I don't think so, for two reasons. First, some towns have a lot of steep hills, and riding up them is very tiring. Second, it's hard to carry heavy shopping on a bike, especially for a big family. Both answers are good answers. Each one gives a clear opinion, two reasons, and the words first and second." What are the two reasons in Answer A? Riding a bike is good for your health, and bikes don't make the air dirty. Does the examiner want one special answer? No. Any opinion is fine if you give reasons. Then we try it. Riko says she thinks so, because new bike lanes are safe and there are fewer traffic jams. Haru says he doesn't think so, because rain makes riding hard. Riko nods. That's a good point. I didn't think about rainy days. Celeste rings softly and says, Two friends, two opinions, and both of them are kind. Now, phonics. Visible has vis, which means see, like vision. Describe has scrib, which means write, like scribble. Audience has aud, which means hear, like audio. Next time, we'll practice what to say when we don't hear a question. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Look at the picture. What is the boy doing? Next time, we'll practice what to say when we don't hear a question. | A+C, objective, objective (US policy) | the quote has no 'says' / 'asks', so the voice reads the character's words as my own; story: Kiko says 'That's a good point. I didn't think about rainy days.' in the conversation (line 5) and seg7, not Riko; the quote also had no 'says'; 'shopping' for the things you bought is British; US 'groceries' |

| 1184 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Do you think more people will ride bikes in the future? Why? Riko and Haru had different opinions, and both of them gave two good reasons. Today, Birimo wants to be the examiner again. Ms. Emma agrees, but there is one problem. Birimo is eating a pancake! He asks Kiko a question, but his mouth is full, and he mumbles. Mmf mmf favorite mmf? Kiko doesn't understand. In a real interview, the examiner won't eat pancakes, but sometimes you won't hear the question. The room might be noisy, or the examiner might speak quickly. That's OK. You can ask politely. Could you say that again, please? Say it with me. Could you say that again, please? Perfect! Asking again isn't a mistake. It shows that you really want to understand. Let's read the tips for interviews. "Tips for Interviews. Tip one: If you didn't hear the question, don't panic. Say, Could you say that again, please? Examiners are happy to repeat. Tip two: If the question is difficult, you can take a little thinking time. Say, Let me see, or That's a good question. Then take a breath. Tip three: If you're not sure, say, I'm not sure, but I think, and then give your best idea. Tip four: Try not to stay silent for a long time. A short answer with a reason is better than no answer. Tip five: Speak clearly and look at the examiner. Don't mumble, and please don't eat pancakes!" What can you say if you didn't hear the question? Could you say that again, please? What can you say when you need thinking time? Let me see, or That's a good question. Then Birimo swallows and asks again clearly. What's your favorite season, and why? Kiko says, Let me see. I like spring, because the flowers come back. Next, Birimo asks Haru a tricky question. If you could have any animal as a friend, which would you choose? Haru says, That's a good question. I'm not sure, but I think I'd choose a dolphin. Now, phonics. Telephone has tele, which means far, like telescope. Paragraph has graph, which means write or draw, like autograph. Geography has geo, which means earth, like geology. Next time, we'll put everything together in a full mock interview. | Hello, everyone! | 1185 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Could you say that again, please? We've practiced every part of the interview: the self-introduction, the picture, the opinion, and asking again. Today is the big day: a mock interview（本番の れんしゅう）. Celeste changes the little room of light. Now there's a door, a desk, a clock and a stack of cards, just like a real test room. Ms. Emma sits behind the desk with a calm smile. At the end, she will say the words that finish every interview. Thank you. That's the end of the test. Say it with me. Thank you. That's the end of the test. Well done! Kiko goes first, and her hands are shaking. Ms. Emma says softly, Being nervous is normal. Take a breath and smile. Kiko knocks, walks in, says good morning and sits up straight. Then Ms. Emma gives her the first card. Let's read the mock interview card. "Mock Interview Card. Please read this passage aloud. Kite Day. Every spring, the town of Brightfield holds a Kite Day by the river. Families bring kites of every shape and color, like fish, dragons and even birds. Children who don't have a kite can make one at a free workshop. Making a kite takes about an hour, and helpers show you each step. Many people say the best part is the moment their kite flies for the first time. Questions. One: Where is Kite Day held? Two: Look at the picture. What are the people doing? Three: Do you think children should spend more time outside? Why? Remember, take a breath, smile and do your best." Where is Kite Day held? By the river. How long does it take to make a kite? About an hour. Kiko reads slowly and clearly. She describes the families flying kites in the picture, and she gives her opinion with two reasons. Then Ms. Emma smiles and says the last words. Haru, Riko and Birimo cheer. Suddenly, the third point of the star begins to shine like warm gold. The Ray of Courage lit up! Celeste says, Being brave doesn't mean you aren't nervous. It means you keep going. Shine on! Now, phonics. Let's review. Inspect has spect, which means look. Erupt has rupt, which means break. Audience has aud, which means hear. Rewrite has re, which means again. Next time, we'll talk about our wishes for the years ahead. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Could you say that again, please?
