| 1186 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Thank you. That's the end of the test. Everyone finished the mock interview, and the third point of the star is shining brightly. Today, Celeste takes us to the Pool of Starlight again. Before, the pool showed us the past. Now Celeste says, This pool can also hold your wishes for the future. Say a wish out loud, and a little light will rise. Riko kneels by the water. She thinks for a moment and says, I hope to visit many countries someday. Say it with me. I hope to visit many countries someday. Beautiful! A tiny blue light floats up from the pool and dances above Riko's head. We use I hope to when we'd really like to do something in the future. Ms. Emma gives each of us a star-shaped card, and we write our wishes on it together. Let's read our hope list. "Our Hope List. Haru: I hope to swim with dolphins someday, because they are my favorite sea animals. I also hope to see a real coral reef. Riko: I hope to visit many countries someday. I want to learn about different cultures and read stories in many languages. Kiko: I hope to play the flute on a big stage with children from other continents. Music is a language everyone understands. Birimo: I hope to taste pancakes in every country in the world. I will need a very big plate. Ms. Emma: I hope to keep learning new things with all of you, every single day." What does Haru hope to see? A real coral reef. Why does Kiko want to play music with other children? Because music is a language everyone understands. As we read each hope aloud, more lights rise from the pool. Soon, the air around us is full of tiny lights, blue and pink and gold. Celeste smiles at Birimo's light, which is shaped like a pancake. Now, phonics. In a speech, we pause after an opening phrase and stress its last important word. Six years aGO, pause. Good morning, EVeryone, pause. When I was SIX, pause. Next time, we'll imagine what we'll be like when we're twenty. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Thank you. That's the end of the test. I give everyone a star-shaped card, and we write our wishes together. Let's read our hope list. "Our Hope List. Haru: I hope to swim with dolphins someday, because they are my favorite sea animals. I also hope to see a real coral reef. Riko: I hope to visit many countries someday. I want to learn about different cultures and read stories in many languages. Kiko: I hope to play the flute on a big stage with children from other continents. Music is a language everyone understands. Birimo: I hope to taste pancakes in every country in the world. I will need a very big plate. Ms. Emma: I hope to keep learning new things with all of you, every single day." What does Haru hope to see? A real coral reef. Why does Kiko want to play music with other children? Because music is a language everyone understands. As we read each hope aloud, more lights rise from the pool. Soon, the air around us is full of tiny lights, blue and pink and gold. Celeste smiles at Birimo's light, which is shaped like a pancake. Now, phonics. In a speech, we pause after an opening phrase and stress its last important word. Six years aGO, pause. Good morning, EVeryone, pause. When I was SIX, pause. Next time, we'll imagine what we'll be like when we're twenty. | A+C (block rule) | Ms. Emma reads her own script, so her own narration "Ms. Emma says / asks / smiles / gives ..." -> "I say / I ask / I smile / I give ..." | 1187 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, I hope to visit many countries someday. The air above the Pool of Starlight was full of tiny lights, one for every hope. Today, Celeste asks us a new question. Close your eyes and imagine you're twenty. What do you see? Birimo jumps up, puts on a pair of round glasses and a fake mustache, and says, I'm playing twenty-year-old Birimo! Everyone laughs. Then Riko closes her eyes and smiles. By the time I'm twenty, I'd like to speak three languages. Say it with me. By the time I'm twenty, I'd like to speak three languages. Great! By the time I'm twenty means before I become twenty, or when I'm twenty. You don't need to use it yourself yet. Just listen for it and understand it. Ms. Emma helps us make a future plan together. Let's read it. "Our Future Plan. Riko: By the time I'm twenty, I'd like to speak three languages. I'll keep reading books in English, and I'll start a new language, too. Haru: When I'm twenty, I'd like to study sea animals at university. I'll probably still go swimming every weekend, even in winter. Kiko: When I'm older, I'd like to teach music to young children. At first, I was shy, but now I love sharing songs. Birimo: When I'm twenty, I'd like to flip ten pancakes at once and share them with everyone on the star. Most of all, we'd like to stay friends, no matter how old we are." What would Haru like to study? Sea animals. What would we like most of all? To stay friends. Kiko says, I'm a little worried. Twenty feels so far away. Ms. Emma nods. It's OK not to know everything now. Dreams can grow and change, just like you. Celeste rings softly, and the starlight in the pool shows four grown-up friends, laughing together. Birimo takes off his mustache. I like being me now, too. Now, phonics. In a speech, we pause after a linking phrase and stress its last word. At FIRST, pause. After a WHILE, pause. Most of ALL, pause. These little pauses help the audience follow your ideas. Next time, we'll talk about how we want to use English in our future. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, I hope to visit many countries someday. | 1188 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, By the time I'm twenty, I'd like to speak three languages. We imagined our future selves, and Birimo looked very funny with his mustache. Today, Celeste waves her tiny arms, and the starlight forms moving pictures in the air. We see a scientist sharing a discovery with people from many countries. We see a doctor helping a traveler who is lost and worried. We see a pilot talking to the airport, and a translator turning a story from one language into another. Celeste says, All of them use English in their careers. But English isn't only for work. It's also for friendship. Kiko watches a picture of children playing music together, and she says, I want to use English to make friends all over the world. Say it with me. I want to use English to make friends all over the world. Wonderful! After I want to use English to, we say what we want to do. Celeste shows us a glowing card. Let's read it. "Ways to Use English. You can use English to make friends all over the world. You can write to a pen pal on another continent, or chat with a new friend at a festival. You can use English to help people. Doctors, nurses and guides use it to help travelers. You can use English to learn. Many science books, websites and videos are in English. You can use English to share. Writers, musicians and scientists share their work with the world. Most of all, you can use English to understand people who are different from you. In the future, no matter what job you choose, English can open doors." Who uses English to help travelers? Doctors, nurses and guides. What can English do in the future, no matter what job you choose? It can open doors. Haru says he wants to use English to share his discoveries about the ocean. Riko wants to use English to read books from other countries, and maybe become a translator. Birimo says, I want to use English to order pancakes everywhere! Ms. Emma laughs. That's a real skill, too. Now, phonics. Thanks to YOU, pause. In the FUture, pause. No matter WHAT, pause. Stress the last important word, then take a short breath. Next time, we'll write letters to ourselves in ten years. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, By the time I'm twenty, I'd like to speak three languages. Doctors, nurses and guides use it to help travelers. You can use English to learn. Many science books, websites and videos are in English. You can use English to share. Writers, musicians and scientists share their work with the world. Most of all, you can use English to understand people who are different from you. In the future, no matter what job you choose, English can open doors." Who uses English to help travelers? Doctors, nurses and guides. What can English do in the future, no matter what job you choose? It can open doors. Haru says he wants to use English to share his discoveries about the ocean. Riko wants to use English to read books from other countries, and maybe become a translator. Birimo says, I want to use English to order pancakes everywhere! I laugh. That's a real skill, too. Now, phonics. Thanks to YOU, pause. In the FUture, pause. No matter WHAT, pause. Stress the last important word, then take a short breath. Next time, we'll write letters to ourselves in ten years. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, By the time I'm twenty, I'd like to speak three languages. We imagined our future selves, and Birimo looked very funny with his mustache. Today, Celeste waves her tiny arms, and the starlight forms moving pictures in the air. We see a scientist sharing a discovery with people from many countries. We see a doctor helping a traveler who is lost and worried. We see a pilot talking to the control tower, and a translator turning a story from one language into another. Celeste says, All of them use English in their careers. But English isn't only for work. It's also for friendship. Kiko watches a picture of children playing music together, and she says, I want to use English to make friends all over the world. Say it with me. | 1189 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, I want to use English to make friends all over the world. We talked about pen pals, translators, scientists and even ordering pancakes. Today, Celeste gives each of us a soft, glowing envelope. She says, Write a letter to yourself in ten years. I'll keep your letters inside the star like a time capsule. In ten years, your letter will find its way back to you. Riko looks at her empty paper and smiles. She writes the first line. Dear me in ten years, I hope you still love learning. Say it with me. Dear me in ten years, I hope you still love learning. Lovely! A letter to your future self can ask questions, remember the past and give some kind advice. Haru lets us read his letter. Let's read it together. "Dear me in ten years, I hope you still love learning. Right now, I'm on the Star of Beginnings with Riko, Kiko, Birimo and Ms. Emma. Looking back, I've come such a long way. Six years ago, I could only say hello. Now, I can give presentations and answer questions in English. Are you studying the ocean now? Have you seen a real coral reef? Do you still swim every weekend? Remember how hard past tense verbs were for me? Went, ate, saw! I made lots of mistakes, but I kept going. So if something is difficult for you now, keep going, too. Before I finish, please say hello to Riko, Kiko and Birimo for me. I hope we're still friends, and I hope we're still laughing together. Shine on! Love, me." What was hard for Haru? Past tense verbs. What does Haru want his future self to say to his friends? Hello. Kiko writes about her flute, and Birimo writes a very short letter: Dear me, are there still pancakes? Love, Birimo. We seal our envelopes, and Celeste carries them gently into the heart of the star. Now, phonics. Before I FINish, pause. Looking BACK, pause. ToGETHer, pause. Say the strong part clearly, then take a short breath before you go on. Next time, the Future Dreams Fair opens on the star! | Hello, everyone! | 1189 | `teacher.segments[21].subtitleJa` | すてきだったよ！ みらいの じぶんに 手紙を かいて、しつもん して、やさしい アドバイスも できるね。セレステが 手紙を 星の なかで だいじに まもって くれるよ。このあと ハルくんと おはなし してね！ バイバイ！ | すてきだったよ！ みらいの じぶんに 手紙を かいて、しつもん して、やさしい アドバイスも できるね。セレストが 手紙を 星の なかで だいじに まもって くれるよ。このあと ハルくんと おはなし してね！ バイバイ！ | objective (JA) | Celeste is セレスト everywhere else in W40 (78 times, and the test name); セレステ is a variant |
| 1190 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Dear me in ten years, I hope you still love learning. Celeste is keeping our letters safe inside the star. Today is the Future Dreams Fair! The silver grass is full of booths made of light, and hundreds of tiny lights from the star float around like curious visitors. Each of us has a booth with a sign. Haru's booth is first. He has drawn a deep blue sea with glowing fish, and he says proudly, In the future, I'd like to study the ocean because I love sea animals. Say it with me. In the future, I'd like to study the ocean because I love sea animals. Fantastic! Let's read the booth signs. "Future Dreams Fair. Booth 1, Haru: In the future, I'd like to study the ocean because I love sea animals. Ever since Deep Sea City, I've wanted to explore places where light never reaches. Booth 2, Riko: I'd like to write children's books and translate stories, so children everywhere can enjoy them. Booth 3, Kiko: I'd like to be a music teacher and play the flute with children around the world. Booth 4, Birimo: I'd like to be a pancake chef on a spaceship. Pancakes taste better with a view of the stars! A note from Ms. Emma: Dreams can change, and that's OK. Keep learning, and your dream will grow with you." Why does Haru want to study the ocean? Because he loves sea animals. What does Ms. Emma say about dreams? Dreams can change, and that's OK. When we read Birimo's sign, everyone bursts out laughing, and Birimo takes a bow. Then Kiko asks Ms. Emma, What if I want a different dream next year? Ms. Emma smiles. Then you'll have a new adventure. Six years ago, you didn't know you'd stand here. Suddenly, the fourth point of the star begins to glow, soft and pink like a sunrise. The Ray of Dreams lit up! Celeste says, Your dreams make the star brighter. Shine on! Now, phonics. Let's review. Six years aGO, pause. Most of ALL, pause. Looking BACK, pause. Predict has dict, which means say. Next time, we'll say thank you to the friends who helped us along the way. | Hello, everyone! | 1191 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, In the future, I'd like to study the ocean because I love sea animals. After the Future Dreams Fair, four rays of the star are shining: Memories, Growth, Courage and Dreams. Two rays are still dim. Celeste rings softly like a tiny bell. Before the next ray shines, she says, think of everyone who helped you. Kiko looks at Haru, Riko and Birimo. She remembers the day she was too nervous to speak, and Riko held her hand. Haru remembers the long words he couldn't read, and Kiko read them with him slowly. Riko remembers getting lost in the Labyrinth of Tales, and Birimo kept everyone laughing. Kiko says, I couldn't have done it without you. Say it with me. I couldn't have done it without you. Wonderful! Celeste gives each of us a glass jar of light. Whisper a thank-you into it, she says, and the light will fly to anyone. Haru writes to our old guides. Let's read them. "Dear Ember, thank you for teaching us to be brave on the Island of Fire. Dear Skye, thank you for showing us the sky and helping us talk with travelers. Dear Olive, thank you for teaching us to share our opinions and listen kindly. Dear Lumi and Dr. Vale, thank you for our first research presentation under the sea. Dear Nib, thank you for reminding us that everyone has a story to tell. Dear Puff and Grandma Rowan, thank you for teaching us to name our feelings, and for the warmest pancakes ever. Dear Orbit, thank you for showing us that every voice counts. We will never forget you. With love and gratitude, Haru, Riko, Kiko and Birimo." Who taught us to name our feelings? Puff and Grandma Rowan. What did Orbit show us? That every voice counts. We whisper into the jars and open the lids. Whoosh! Tiny lights fly away like shooting stars. Far away, a tiny blue light blinks back. Maybe it's Lumi! Now, phonics. Right starts with R, so your tongue doesn't touch anything. Light starts with L, so your tongue touches behind your top teeth. Read starts with R, too, but lead starts with L. Very starts with V. Put your top teeth on your lower lip. Berry starts with B, with your lips together. Next time, we'll plan a secret surprise for someone very special. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, In the future, I'd like to study the ocean because I love sea animals. After the Future Dreams Fair, four rays of the star are shining: Memories, Growth, Courage and Dreams. Two rays are still dim. Celeste rings softly like a tiny bell. Before the next ray shines, she says, think of everyone who helped you. Kiko looks at Haru, Riko and Birimo. She remembers the day she was too nervous to speak, and Riko held her hand. Haru remembers the long words he couldn't read, and Kiko read them with him slowly. Riko remembers getting lost in the Labyrinth of Tales, and Birimo kept everyone laughing. Kiko says, I couldn't have done it without you. Say it with me. I couldn't have done it without you. Wonderful! Celeste gives each of us a glass jar of light. Whisper a thank-you into it, she says, and the light will fly to anyone. Haru writes to our old guides. Let's read them. "Dear Ember, thank you for teaching us to be brave on the Island of Fire. Dear Skye, thank you for showing us the sky and helping us talk with travelers. Dear Olive, thank you for teaching us to share our opinions and listen kindly. Dear Lumi and Dr. Vale, thank you for our first research presentation under the sea. Dear Nib, thank you for reminding us that everyone has a story to tell. Dear Puff and Grandma Rowan, thank you for teaching us to name our feelings, and for the warmest pancakes ever. Dear Orbit, thank you for showing us that every voice counts. We will never forget you. With love and gratitude, Haru, Riko, Kiko and Birimo." Who taught us to name our feelings? Puff and Grandma Rowan. What did Orbit show us? That every voice counts. We whisper into the jars and open the lids. Whoosh! Tiny lights fly away like shooting stars. Far away, a tiny blue light blinks back. Maybe it's Lumi! Now, phonics. Right starts with R, so your tongue doesn't touch anything. Light starts with L, so your tongue touches behind your top teeth. Read starts with R, too, but lead, as in lead the way, starts with L. Very starts with V. Put your top teeth on your lower lip. Berry starts with B, with your lips together. Next time, we'll plan a secret surprise for someone very special. | B+C | 'lead' is a heteronym (led / leed); give the verb context (same as seg9) |
| 1192 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, I couldn't have done it without you. This morning, Riko has an idea. She whispers, We thanked our friends and our guides, but who has been with us every single day for six years? Ms. Emma! Let's make a surprise for her. Birimo's job is to keep Ms. Emma busy. He holds up a spoon like a microphone and asks, Ms. Emma, how do you make the fluffiest pancakes in the world? Do you flip them once or twice? What about blueberries? Ms. Emma laughs and starts to explain. Behind a silver bush, Haru, Riko and Kiko make a card with ribbons and stickers. Kiko writes the most important line. Thank you for always believing in us. Say it with me. Thank you for always believing in us. Beautiful! When Birimo runs out of questions, we all shout, Surprise! Let's read the card. "Dear Ms. Emma, six years ago, we met you in the Forest of Beginnings. We could only say hello, but you smiled as if we were already great speakers. You taught us four magic words, and then thousands more. When we made mistakes, you said, Mistakes help us grow. When we were nervous, you stood right next to us. You read stories with us, sang songs with us, and laughed at all of Birimo's jokes, even the very silly ones. Thanks to you, we can read books, tell stories and share our ideas in English. Thank you for always believing in us. You are the best teacher in every world. With love, Haru, Riko, Kiko and Birimo. P.S. Birimo really wants your pancake recipe." Where did we first meet Ms. Emma? In the Forest of Beginnings. What did Ms. Emma say when we made mistakes? Mistakes help us grow. Ms. Emma reads the card twice. Her eyes get shiny, and a happy tear rolls down her cheek. She hugs all four of us. These are happy tears, she says. I'm so proud of you. Now, phonics. Vest starts with V, so put your top teeth on your lower lip. Best starts with B, with your lips together. Think starts with TH. Put your tongue between your teeth and blow. Sink starts with S, with your tongue behind your teeth. Three is TH plus R. Free starts with F, and tree starts with T. Next time, we'll start writing a very important speech. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, I couldn't have done it without you. This morning, Riko has an idea. | 1193 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Thank you for always believing in us. Today, Celeste has big news. In one week, we will have our graduation here on the Star of Beginnings, and one student will give the graduation speech. Who should it be? Haru says, I vote for Kiko! She used to be so nervous, but now she speaks so clearly. Riko and Birimo raise their hands, too. Kiko's eyes go wide. Me? She is a little scared, but she says yes, as long as everyone helps her. We sit in a circle and make an outline: the past, the journey, thank-yous and the future. Kiko thinks for a while. Then she writes the very first line. Six years ago, I didn't know any English. Say it with me. Six years ago, I didn't know any English. Lovely! Then everyone adds one line. Let's read the first draft. "Six years ago, I didn't know any English. I was shy, and I was afraid of making mistakes. Then, in the Forest of Beginnings, I met my friends and my teacher, and our journey began. At first, we could only say hello. After a while, we could ask questions, read books and tell stories. We visited many worlds and met many wonderful guides. Haru taught us that mistakes help us learn. Riko taught us that every person has a story worth sharing. Birimo taught us that laughing together makes hard things easier. Most of all, Ms. Emma taught us to believe in ourselves. Looking back, I am proud of how much we have grown. Graduation is not the last page of our story. Together, we will keep reading, keep singing and keep talking in English. Thank you, everyone." Who taught us that every person has a story worth sharing? Riko. What did Ms. Emma teach us? To believe in ourselves. Birimo asks, Can I add a line about pancakes? Everyone laughs. Maybe in the next draft, Birimo! Now, phonics. She starts with SH, so round your lips and push the air out. See starts with S, with a smile. Fall starts with F. Put your top teeth on your lower lip. Hall starts with H, which is just air. Hat has a wide sound, so open your mouth wide. Hut has a short, relaxed sound. Next time, Kiko will practice her speech in front of all of us. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Thank you for always believing in us. Today, Celeste has big news. | 1194 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Six years ago, I didn't know any English. Today is rehearsal day. Celeste waves her tiny arms, and the silver grass rises into a small round stage with a soft spotlight of starlight. Kiko walks up with her speech in her hands. The rest of us sit in front of the stage. Birimo sits in the back row and shouts, I'm playing the audience! All of it! Kiko begins. Six years ago, I didn't know any English. But she reads very fast and looks only at her paper. When she finishes, she sighs. Oh no, I went way too fast. Ms. Emma smiles and points to three places in the speech. Pause here. Smile. Look at the audience. Say it with me. Pause here. Smile. Look at the audience. Excellent! Then Ms. Emma gives Kiko a little card. Let's read the rehearsal tips. "Rehearsal Tips. One: Stand up straight. Good posture helps your voice sound strong. Two: Speak slowly. When we are nervous, we often speak too fast, so slow down a little more than you think. Three: Pause here. After an important line, stop for a moment and let your words reach everyone. Four: Smile. A smile helps you relax, and it helps the audience relax, too. Five: Look at the audience. Make eye contact with a few friendly faces, near and far. Six: It's OK to be nervous. Even great speakers feel butterflies in their stomachs. Take a deep breath and remember why your words matter. Last tip: Practice, practice, practice, and then enjoy it!" Why should you smile? It helps you and the audience relax. What can you do when you feel nervous? Take a deep breath. Kiko tries again. This time, she speaks slowly, pauses after six years ago, and looks right at Birimo in the back row. When she finishes, we all clap and cheer. Birimo says, I heard every word! I got goosebumps! Kiko laughs. She is still nervous, but now she is excited, too. Now, phonics. World has the ER sound and then an L. Word has no L. Walk has an aw sound, and the L is silent. Work has the ER sound. Heard has ER, too, but hard has AR, so open your mouth wide. Next time, Celeste will tell us a story that will surprise everyone, especially Birimo. | Hello, everyone! | 1195 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Pause here. Smile. Look at the audience. Tonight, we all sit around the Pool of Starlight. Five rays of the star are shining above us. Celeste looks at Birimo with the same gentle smile she has given him since the first day. Then she rings softly and says, It's time for a story. A true story. Let's read Celeste's story. "Six years ago, a tiny light lived here on the Star of Beginnings. One morning, it looked down through the clouds and saw a quiet green forest. Children were starting their journey with words there, and some of them looked a little shy. The tiny light wanted to help them. So it floated down, down, down, all the way to the Forest of Beginnings, and it became a small, playful creature who loved to laugh. It carried a piece of starlight in its heart. That is why its map glowed, and why the map always knew the way. It stayed close to the children, cheered them on, and made them laugh on hard days. Do you know who that tiny light was? It was Birimo." Everyone turns to Birimo. His cheeks turn pink. Haru whispers, So Birimo had been watching over us all along. Say it with me. Birimo had been watching over us all along. Wonderful! Why did the tiny light float down? To help the children starting their journey. Why did the map always know the way? Because Birimo carried a piece of starlight in his heart. Birimo smiles shyly. I forgot! I was having so much fun with you that I forgot where I came from. I just loved being your friend. Celeste says, You may come home to the star now, little light. Everyone is quiet. Birimo looks at us and shakes his head. My home is wherever my friends are! Celeste laughs like a little bell and says, Then stay with them. Shine on! Suddenly, the fifth ray begins to glow, warm and golden. The Ray of Thanks lit up! Now, phonics. Let's review. Very starts with V, with your top teeth on your lower lip. Think starts with TH, with your tongue between your teeth. World has ER and then L. Thanks to you: stress YOU, then pause. Thanks to you, I had the best six years. Next time, we can talk about anything we like! | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Pause here. Smile. Look at the audience. Tonight, we all sit around the Pool of Starlight. Five rays of the star are shining above us. Celeste looks at Birimo with the same gentle smile she has given him since the first day. Then she rings softly and says, It's time for a story. | 1196 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Birimo had been watching over us all along. This morning, Birimo is still Birimo. He wakes up first, does a happy dance on the silver grass and asks if stars have pancakes for breakfast. We all laugh. Some things will never change, and we're glad. Ms. Emma brings out a big colorful wheel. Today is free-talk day, she says. There are no tests and no scripts. Just friends talking. Let's talk about anything you like! Say it with me. Let's talk about anything you like! Great! Ms. Emma points to the card under the wheel. Let's read it. "The Free-Talk Topic Wheel. Spin the wheel and talk about the topic it lands on: hobbies, food, books, dreams, funny memories or your favorite worlds. Here are three tips for great free talk. First, answer with more than one sentence. Don't just say, Yes, I do. Add a reason or an example. Second, ask a follow-up question, like, Why do you like it? or, What happened next? Third, react to your friend. Say things like, No way!, That's awesome!, or, Same here! A conversation is like playing catch. You throw a question, your friend catches it and throws something back. If you drop the ball, just pick it up and smile. There are no wrong topics today. Enjoy it!" What should you add after a short answer? A reason or an example. What is a conversation like? Playing catch. Birimo spins first, and of course, the wheel lands on food. Haru gets funny memories, and he tells the story of the day Birimo tried to fly a kite in the rain. Riko gets books, and Kiko asks her three follow-up questions in a row. Celeste listens from the top of the wheel and rings happily every time someone says, No way! At first, free talk felt hard, but now our words come out easily, like water from a spring. Now, phonics. Let's review. Play ends with ay. Dislike is dis plus like, and dis means the opposite. Construct has struct, which means build. At first: stress FIRST, then pause. At first, I was shy. Read starts with R, so your tongue doesn't touch anything. Next time, we'll tell the story of our six-year journey. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Birimo had been watching over us all along. This morning, Birimo is still Birimo. | 1197 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Let's talk about anything you like! Today, Celeste has a special request. Every journey that begins here becomes a story, she says, and this star keeps those stories. Will you tell me yours? Riko jumps up. She loves telling stories more than anything. She thinks for a moment, smiles, and begins with the oldest words in storytelling. Once upon a time, four friends found a map. Say it with me. Once upon a time, four friends found a map. Fantastic! Haru adds the middle, Kiko adds the feelings, and Birimo adds the sound effects. Then Ms. Emma writes it all down. Let's read our story. "Once upon a time, four friends found a map. Well, actually, they met long before that, in the Forest of Beginnings, when they could only say hello. One day, in the Kingdom of English, Birimo found an old map of worlds that were not on any map yet. The map began to glow, and with their teacher, the friends followed it from world to world. They crossed bridges, climbed mountains, rode trains above the clouds and traveled deep under the sea. They solved riddles, performed plays, invented machines and wrote their own stories. Sometimes the journey was hard, and they made lots of mistakes. But they helped each other with kindness, and they kept going. At last, a beam of light carried them up to a star, where every journey begins. And the story? It isn't finished yet." Where did Birimo find the map? In the Kingdom of English. What did the friends do when the journey was hard? They helped each other and kept going. Celeste listens with her eyes closed. When the story ends, she lowers the pages gently into the Pool of Starlight. The words turn into tiny lights and form a new chapter in the water. A good story has a beginning, a middle and an ending, Ms. Emma says, but the best stories leave room for more. Now, phonics. Let's review. Green has double e. | 1198 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Once upon a time, four friends found a map. Today, Celeste asks us a big question. Why do people learn languages? Ms. Emma says this is a perfect question for an opinion speech. A good opinion speech has four parts. First, say your opinion. Next, give your reasons. Then, give an example. Finally, say your opinion again. We each write a short speech. Riko goes first. She stands tall, smiles at the audience and says, I believe that learning languages connects people. Say it with me. I believe that learning languages connects people. Excellent! Let's read Riko's speech. "I believe that learning languages connects people. I have two reasons. First, when you speak someone's language, even a little, they smile and open their hearts. Second, learning a language helps you understand how other people think and feel. Here is an example. At the World Round Table, I met Priya, who lives on another continent. At first, we were both shy, and it seemed impossible to become friends. But we tried English together. She taught me a song from her home, and I taught her a game from mine. Soon we were laughing like old friends. Language made the invisible wall between us disappear. No matter what language you learn, it can build a bridge to someone new. That's why I believe that learning languages connects people." How many reasons does Riko give? Two. What made the invisible wall disappear? Language. Then Haru speaks. He believes that languages help us learn about the world, because there are amazing books and ideas in every language. Kiko believes that music and words together can reach any heart. Birimo stands up proudly. I believe that pancakes connect people! Everyone laughs, but then he gives a good reason. When you share food, you share stories, too. Ms. Emma nods. That's actually a good point. Now, phonics. Let's review. Shirt has ir, which sounds the same as er and ur. Impossible is im plus possible, and im means not before a p. Visible has vis, which means see. No matter what: stress WHAT, then pause. No matter what happens, keep learning. Walk has a silent L. Next time, it will be the night before our graduation. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Once upon a time, four friends found a map. Today, Celeste asks us a big question. | 1199 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, I believe that learning languages connects people. Tonight is graduation eve. Celeste dims the floating lights, and the sky above the star fills with more stars than we can count. We lie down on the soft silver grass with warm blankets, side by side, and look up. Nobody talks for a while. Then Haru says, I can't believe this is our last night before graduation. Kiko says she feels a little sad, because something special is ending. Riko says she feels excited, too. Birimo yawns a huge yawn and says, I'm sleepy... Everyone laughs, because that is exactly what he said in our very first week. Ms. Emma sits up and smiles at us. It's OK to feel sad and excited at the same time, she says. Tomorrow, a new journey begins. Say it with me. Tomorrow, a new journey begins. Beautiful! Then Ms. Emma gives each of us a small card. Let's read the goodnight page. "A Goodnight Page. Tonight, close your eyes and remember. Remember the first time you said hello, and the first book you read all the way to the end. Remember the riddles, the songs, the plays and the stories you wrote. Remember the days when English felt hard, and how you kept going anyway. It's OK to feel a little sad tonight. Something special is ending. It's also OK to feel excited, because something new is starting. Tomorrow, you will graduate, but learning never really stops. There will always be new books to read, new songs to sing and new friends to talk with. So sleep well, my brave learners. Tomorrow, a new journey begins. With love, Ms. Emma." Why is it OK to feel excited? Because something new is starting. What will there always be? New books, new songs and new friends. Just then, a shooting star flies across the sky, and we all make a wish. Now, phonics. Let's review. Knee has a silent k. Preview is pre plus view, and pre means before. Telephone has tele, which means far. In the future: stress FU, then pause. In the future, I'll keep reading. Fall starts with F, so put your top teeth on your lower lip. Hall is just air. Next time is our graduation day, the very last lesson of our six years! | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, I believe that learning languages connects people. Tonight is graduation eve. Celeste dims the floating lights, and the sky above the star fills with more stars than we can count. We lie down on the soft silver grass with warm blankets, side by side, and look up. Nobody talks for a while. Then Haru says, I can't believe this is our last night before graduation. Kiko says she feels a little sad, because something special is ending. Riko says she feels excited, too. Birimo yawns a huge yawn and says, I'm sleepy... Everyone laughs, because that is exactly what he said in our very first week. I sit up and smile at everyone. I say, It's OK to feel sad and excited at the same time. Tomorrow, a new journey begins. Say it with me. Tomorrow, a new journey begins. Beautiful! Then I give everyone a small card. Let's read the goodnight page. "A Goodnight Page. Tonight, close your eyes and remember. Remember the first time you said hello, and the first book you read all the way to the end. Remember the riddles, the songs, the plays and the stories you wrote. Remember the days when English felt hard, and how you kept going anyway. It's OK to feel a little sad tonight. Something special is ending. It's also OK to feel excited, because something new is starting. Tomorrow, you will graduate, but learning never really stops. There will always be new books to read, new songs to sing and new friends to talk with. So sleep well, my brave learners. Tomorrow, a new journey begins. With love, Ms. Emma." Why is it OK to feel excited? Because something new is starting. What will there always be? New books, new songs and new friends. | 1200 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Tomorrow, a new journey begins. Today is the day! This is our 1,200th lesson, and it's graduation day on the Star of Beginnings. The silver grass sparkles, and the Pool of Starlight shows our families far below, watching and waving. Then little lights fly up from every world. Orbit, Puff, Nib, Lumi, Olive, Skye and Ember have all come to say congratulations! Celeste rings her bell, and Kiko walks to the front. She pauses, smiles and looks at the audience. Let's read her speech. "Six years ago, I didn't know any English. Today, I am standing here with my best friends. When I was little, I was too shy to speak. Now, I can share my ideas with the whole world. We made lots of mistakes, but we never gave up, and we were never alone. Thank you, Haru, for making us brave. Thank you, Riko, for your wonderful stories. Thank you, Birimo, for watching over us and making us laugh. Thank you, Ms. Emma, for always believing in us. And thank you to every guide in every world. Today, we graduate, but our learning doesn't stop. This is not the end. It's a new beginning!" Who did Kiko thank for making us laugh? Birimo. Does our learning stop today? No, it doesn't! Everyone cheers. Ms. Emma gives each of us a graduation certificate and a tiny star pin. Birimo says his pin looks a little like a pancake. Then Ms. Emma says, One last time, our four magic words! Look! Stop! Come here! Go! Suddenly, the sixth ray begins to shine. The Ray of Beginnings lit up! All six rays are shining, and the star shines brighter than ever before. Celeste gives us a brand-new map, but it is completely blank. The places will appear as you keep learning, she says. Haru smiles. This is not the end. It's a new beginning! Say it with me. This is not the end. It's a new beginning! Now, phonics. Let's review six years of sounds. Light has igh, and your tongue touches behind your top teeth. Comfortable is comfort plus able. Paragraph has graph, which means write. Together: stress GETH, then pause. Together, we grew. Right starts with R, so your tongue doesn't touch anything. Ms. Emma says, Keep reading stories, singing songs and talking every day. Celeste rings one last time. Shine on! | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Tomorrow, a new journey begins. Today is the day! This is our 1,200th lesson, and it's graduation day on the Star of Beginnings. Celeste gives us a brand-new map, but it is completely blank. The places will appear as you keep learning, she says. Haru smiles. This is not the end. It's a new beginning! Say it with me. This is not the end. It's a new beginning! Now, phonics. Let's review six years of sounds. Light has igh, and your tongue touches behind your top teeth. Comfortable is comfort plus able. Paragraph has graph, which means write. Together: stress GETH, then pause. Together, we grew. Right starts with R, so your tongue doesn't touch anything. I say, Keep reading stories, singing songs and talking every day. Celeste rings one last time. Shine on! | A+C (block rule) | Ms. Emma reads her own script, so her own narration "Ms. Emma says / asks / smiles / gives ..." -> "I say / I ask / I smile / I give ..."
